## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria  

### 17. The Secretariat considers the proposal to be strongly aligned with GCF investment criteria.  

#### 3.1 Impact potential  
Scale: Medium to high  

#### 18. The proposal aims to increase the resilience to climate change of 2,072,571 people living in southern Peru, including major cities such as Cusco, Arequipa, Abancay and Cañete, who will experience improvements in ecosystem services, most notably water quality and availability, generated by the improvement of ecosystems that contribute to water regulation in the upper and lower parts of watersheds, protecting against increased risks of floods and droughts as a result of climate change.  

#### 19. The project is expected to have 60,715 direct beneficiaries, particularly among Quechua-speaking Indigenous Peoples living in the highly vulnerable Southern High Andes region of Peru, will be directly supported by GCF funding, which will support (1) the strengthening of key value chains such as potatoes, quinoa and camelids, and (2) the implementation of EbA activities based on best practices and ancestral knowledge on an estimated 23,914 ha of puna ecosystems that are highly valuable to local communities, enhancing the provision of ecosystem services.  

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift  
Scale: Medium to high  

#### 20. The project presents a strong theory of change responding to the threats to Puna ecosystems and the communities that depend on them. The theory of change focuses on transforming agricultural and animal husbandry value chains by addressing barriers related to access to finance, access to knowledge, and governance and institutional coordination.  

#### 21. The project will expand EbA approaches, so far piloted but not demonstrated at scale, by establishing financial mechanisms to channel public finance via the Puna Facility (with funding windows and instruments tailored to different sets of beneficiaries) and payments for ecosystem service mechanisms. Further, the project will give the capacity to community-level institutions to develop business plans, and the microcredit financial sector to develop tailored financial products for EbA approaches. Parallel development of both public and private finance sources is vital to the exit strategy and scaling-up of the project’s results.  

#### 22. The project features a sound approach to knowledge management based on capturing ancestral knowledge, community monitoring, and learning lessons to identify and promote best practices in EbA. MIDAGRI and Ministry of the Environment personnel, in charge of national programmes and rural extension services, will be trained on sustainable management and traditional practices and tasked with knowledge dissemination to final beneficiaries.

# GCF/B.38/02/Add.06

### 23. The programme has highly significant environmental, social and economic co-benefits, and is closely aligned with several of Peru’s objectives in relation to the Sustainable Development Goals.

### 24. Environmental co-benefits are particularly relevant as a result of the project’s ecosystem-based approach. The restoration and sustainable management of 23,914 ha will improve ecosystem connectivity and the provision of a broad range of ecosystem services – from biodiversity to water-flow management and soil protection – that are particularly critical to the livelihoods of target communities. The sustainable management of ecosystems will also result in a reduction of greenhouse gas emissions, estimated at 407,000 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over 15 years.

### 25. Project activities are expected to yield significant social and economic benefits as a consequence of the strengthening of producer associations and value chains, community decision-making with a gender-mainstreaming approach, and the preservation of cultural values, most importantly through the preservation of ancestral landscape practices. The project is expected to contribute to job and income generation, with a target of increased incomes for over 42,000 beneficiaries.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient  
#### Scale: High  

### 26. Despite Peru’s status as a middle-income country, the project targets predominantly Indigenous Peoples’ communities in isolated areas of the Southern High Andes (69 per cent of beneficiaries are Quechua Indigenous Peoples). In addition to severe vulnerability to climate change, particularly droughts and the availability of natural resources in fragile high-altitude ecosystems, the region ranks among the most impoverished in Peru, with a high incidence of poverty, low rates of access to basic services, and well-documented barriers to access to finance or markets.

### 27. As mentioned as part of the discussion on paradigm shift, the proposal takes on a comprehensive approach to tackling these needs, strengthening value chains, connectedness, governance and use of resources, and ultimately the Puna ecosystems which form the basis of the livelihoods in the region.

### 3.5 Country ownership  
#### Scale: High  

### 28. The programme is part of Peru’s country programme and is well-aligned with both the Peruvian nationally determined contributions on agriculture and water, national adaptation plan, as well as sectoral policies on agriculture and green financing. The programme was developed in close consultation with beneficiaries, including Indigenous Peoples, and with a full range of stakeholders at national, regional and local levels.

### 29. The project will be implemented mostly through Profonanpe, which is an AE to GCF and has ample experience in the operation of similar projects combining funding facilities and the provision of technical assistance, with community-based action in the areas of forestry and agriculture. The project will also benefit from the strong involvement as EEs of SERNANP (in charge of protected areas), IdM (non-profit focused on the conservation of the values of mountain peoples and ecosystems) and MIDAGRI, which will leverage strong on-the-ground capabilities and contribute substantial co-financing.

# GCF/B.38/02/Add.06  
## Page 132  

30. The funding proposal package presents a well-developed economic and financial analysis with clear definition of scope and well-referenced assumptions. It covers the economic and financial performance of the Puna Facility, including repayable, non-repayable and Agroideas window, which takes 47 per cent of the total GCF budget. The remaining 53 per cent of the funding is to be used for technical assistance and capacity-building, and therefore is not taken into account in the economic and financial analysis.  

31. The economic analysis uses a social discount rate of 6 per cent, based on World Bank’s report (2022) on Peru’s climate investment. The results show the net present value of the project as EUR 11.9 million and the economic internal rate of return as 8.2 per cent over a reasonable 17-year time horizon, confirming that the proposed project is economically viable. However, if the discount rate is increased, for example to 8 per cent, which is used in other public sources such as the Ministry of Economy and Finance, the economic net present value is reduced significantly to EUR 0.8 million.  

32. Financial adequacy and appropriateness of concessionality is demonstrated in the financial analysis. The AE has conducted a scenario analysis testing financial results with different financial instruments (loan). The financial model shows mixed results: it is still financially viable for the interventions under the repayable windows and the financial internal rate of return is above the weighted average cost of capital. The AE complements the results with a narrative justification for the need of grants. For the rest of the interventions, loans would not make the project viable. As cash shortfall is quite significant during the project implementation period, it would be difficult for beneficiaries to pay back during the project period. Grants would be the most appropriate instrument.  

33. In terms of the financial sustainability beyond the project period, the key to the sustainability of the Puna Facility lies in the feasibility of mobilizing resources from the private sector, which is the purpose of subactivity 2.1.1.2. To demonstrate such feasibility, the AE was requested to conduct a cash flow scenario analysis, incorporating business models and estimated profits to be achieved by the end of the project, so that the private sector can be attracted and incentivized to get involved in the proposed initiative. The AE analysed each value chain under a business-as-usual scenario and climate-resilient (with-the-project) scenario, where all climate-resilient scenarios show revenue growth and net cash flows.  

34. The repayable grants can also be an instrument for the financial sustainability of the project. The financial model estimates repayment rate as 50 per cent, assuming that different local initiatives may be able to repay in full or in part. The repayment stops in year 6, and after the project completion, the revolving funds may flow back into any of the windows under the Puna Facility, leaving uncertainty on how much funding would be reinvested, their impacts and sustainability beyond the project. The AE responded that Profonanpe will continue windows 2 and 3, under the same terms and conditions for the investment.  

35. The cost-effectiveness is assessed to be medium. The project’s adaptation cost is EUR 672/direct beneficiary and USD 20/total beneficiary. Cost per direct beneficiary is at the higher range of adaptation portfolio in the Latin America and Caribbean region, as the average cost/direct beneficiary in the region (single country projects only) stands at USD 348. The co-financing ratio is 1:0.92, with the sources entirely from the public sector.

Andean ecosystems and community livelihoods. Activities are intended to reduce the degree of losses from climate impacts, improve communities’ relationship with ecosystems, increase their participation in the value chain and product transformation, improve their associative processes and market access, and monitor progress on decreasing their vulnerability. The project will finance the implementation of EbA measures and build CRVCs to restore and conserve the puna ecosystems. The project will also improve structural conditions that will enable rural communities in the High Andes to use and manage the Puna ecosystems in a more sustainable and climate-smart way, offering alternatives to reduce the unsustainable practices that are contributing to ecosystem degradation and magnifying climate change impacts. Among the environmental and social (E&S) co-benefits are the maintenance of ecosystem services in the project area, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, improved local economy and mainstreaming gender-responsive approaches in EbA and CRVC measures.  

### 37. Environmental and social risk category  
The AE assigned the project the moderate E&S risk category, which is considered equivalent of GCF category B. The Secretariat confirms the proposed E&S risk category, considering that the project will largely have positive E&S impacts on the ecosystems and the communities in the SHAP. Nevertheless, investments for EbA and CRVC measures (component 1) may have potential adverse E&S impacts, which will be site-specific, reversible or easily managed through standard mitigation measures. The Secretariat confirms that the assigned risk category is within the accreditation level of the AE.  

### 38. Safeguards instrument  
The AE has provided an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) with the funding proposal as the safeguard document. The document provides an overview of the national context for the E&S risk assessment, the potential E&S risks likely to be associated with the project activities and a range of EbA measures and CRVC interventions targeted by the project. As the project will mobilize public and private financing through the establishment of the Puna Facility to support local initiatives led by the IPLCs, site-specific ESIAs and ESMPs will be developed during the project’s implementation.  

### 39. Compliance with the GCF ESS standards  
The following paragraphs provide a brief description of the programme’s compliance with ESS standards.  

### 40. ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts  
The ESIA has discussed the E&S baseline of the districts and regions in the SHAP targeted by the project and identified the potential E&S risks and impacts against the proposed EbA measures and CRVC interventions along with the indicative mitigation measures detailed in the project’s ESMP. Most of the risks and impacts identified by the AE are of moderate scale and can be readily managed and mitigated through the appropriate procedures and plans as described in the ESMP.  

It is worth noting that the AE has identified that there may be pre-existing or project-induced conflict developing from the participatory process for engaging beneficiaries and supporting the design of intervention plans where EbA measures and CRVCs are prioritized for future local initiatives. Identified conflict risks are related to the exclusion of disadvantaged and vulnerable groups from the potential beneficiaries, competition for access to project grants, use of limited resources (including for downstream water users) and ownership and land tenure.  

To mitigate such risks, the AE will develop and implement an appropriate gender and culturally sensitive beneficiary engagement and communication strategy to ensure that the potential beneficiaries are properly identified. In the context of the Puna Facility, where financial instruments will be applied to support the implementation of local initiatives, because the potential E&S risks and impacts of such financing cannot be assessed in detail in advance, the ESIA outlines the general E&S due diligence principles and the E&S risk management procedures to be followed by activities supported under the Puna Facility. The facility-specific E&S due diligence and screening checklist will be developed during the project inception on the basis of the existing environmental, social and governance screening checklist established by the EE.

### 41. ESS 2: Labour and working conditions  
The ESIA identifies that the project may involve potential low risks of accidents or contagion during the construction and land work of some EbA measures and CRVC interventions, where extensive manual labour may be involved. As no large-scale and long-lasting interventions are foreseen within the Puna Facility, the potential occupational and safety risks are expected to be low and limited to the phase of local initiative implementation. The AE will require site-specific labour management procedures, including health and safety measures and safety protocol, in each local initiative to ensure the provision of protective equipment, when necessary, following the code of ethics and implementation protocol of the EEs.

### 42. ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution control  
The ESIA identifies that the project may involve low risks related to greenhouse gas emissions if the proposed CRVC interventions involve mechanization measures to increase productivity or processing, and accessing markets abroad. The AE also identifies a low risk of pollution concerning the mismanagement of water resources in the watershed in the case of incorrectly designed irrigation and drainage systems. The AE will mitigate such risks by following the eligibility criteria to select proper EbA and CRVC interventions, promoting access to national markets, conducting site-specific E&S due diligence, and providing technical assistance at the local landscape level on managing the project’s environmental risks.

### 43. ESS 4: Community health, safety and security  
The AE identifies that the project may expose local communities to low risks and impacts on their health and safety, including the exposure to potential accidents and accidents during the construction and land work and the potential conflicts over ownership and inappropriate selection or implementation of local interventions and initiatives. The project will require safety protocols specific to each funded local initiative to be developed and implemented to mitigate such risks. Since the project will be locally implemented at the community level and by the community members, with support from local civil society, it is likely that no specific security arrangements will be made for project activities.

### 44. ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement  
The project does not include any activities that will require land acquisition through expropriation or other compulsory procedures. The EbA measures and CRVC activities that will be funded by the project will be designed by communities and community entities and the lands to be used for the funded activities will be in the possession of the community or used by the community. Nevertheless, the ESIA identifies the need to conduct due diligence on lands where any activity or civil works may take place to ensure that the land belongs to the community, the community is in agreement on the proposed use of the land, the land is clear of public/private ownership or use and the benefits of the activity or civil works to be conducted are distributed equitably across the community. In terms of the potential risks and impacts on involuntary resettlement, the ESIA has assessed that there is a risk that project activities could create restrictions on land use and/or access to natural resources. The ESIA also notes the need to conduct community consultations to understand the potential losses (and whether they fall disproportionately on some people in the community) and develop consensus on alternative access, use and/or livelihoods opportunities. The ESIA and the ESMP require that landowners’ titles be confirmed and that the users of any selected local initiatives site to be funded by the Puna Facility ensure the consent of the entire community through the communal assembly and agreement signed.

### 45. ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources  
The AE has identified low risks and impacts on biodiversity as the project aims to improve the conservation of natural resources and their climate-resilient use. The creation of improved road access may facilitate greater access to the public and thus accelerate the degradation of sensitive areas; there could be unexpected damage to wetlands due to an incorrect assessment of the situation; there could be overgrazing due to an incorrect choice on the CRVC component; agroforestry and reforestation may attract new pests and diseases into...

## 46. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples  
The funding proposal seeks to support, through a grant facility and accompanying technical support, investments led both by Indigenous Peoples and by local communities for resilient ecosystems at the local landscape level and strengthening CRVCs with access to markets. It acknowledges the many Indigenous Peoples within the project area and the differential impacts of the project on these diverse groups. An IPLC engagement plan has been provided. There is potential for benefits to Indigenous Peoples, including to the promotion of their knowledge systems. In line with their roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and EEs, who are also encouraged to share emerging good practices and success stories with them.  

## 47. ESS 8: Cultural heritage  
The project aims to restore ancient practices and promote the recovery of ancestral practices in the Puna system. The AE has assessed that the project’s presence presents limited risks for cultural heritage sites, especially in the southern part of Peru as the centre of the ancient Inca Empire. To mitigate such risks, the project will request a Certificate of Non-existence of Archaeological Remains where needed. There is also a risk of unexpected findings of archaeological remains and artefacts emerging during land work. In such a case, the project will inform the Ministry of Culture and request it to assess the findings and define appropriate measures to avoid loss of access to cultural heritage. If a cultural heritage site is found in the local initiatives, the AE will ensure that unexpected findings are reported to the Ministry of Culture and local stakeholders and that chance find procedures are followed.   

## 48. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) safeguarding  
The ESIA has identified that the project poses potential gender-based violence and SEAH risks as part of the AE human rights due diligence and gender analysis in the context of project-supported activities and funded local initiatives. To prevent the project from exacerbating SEAH risks, the AE will implement a zero-tolerance policy on SEAH and operate a survivor-centred gender-responsive and culturally sensitive grievance redress mechanism (GRM) to redress SEAH allegations. Upon obtaining their consent, the project will promptly refer the survivor to the relevant gender-based violence services, including legal, psychosocial, medical care, safety and security related support. The AE will also ensure confidential reporting and monitoring of SEAH incidents. The AE is encouraged to incorporate a SEAH risk assessment as part of the E&S due diligence to be conducted for funded local initiatives under the Puna Facility and to ensure that all project stakeholders have access to the dedicated SEAH GRM as well as the GRMs established by the AE and EEs at the institutional level to redress the project-related SEAH allegations.

The AE will also be responsible for ensuring the project’s compliance with the E&S safeguards standards, the ESMP, the gender action plan and the IPLC engagement plan. GIZ Peru will act as the technical secretariat within the Project Steering Committee and as the executive coordination within the Project Management Committee as well as the Territorial

### Implementation Unit  
Profonanpe, in its capacity of one of the project’s EEs, will be responsible for the management of the Puna Facility under the supervision of the AE. Profonanpe will set up a Facility Management Unit to ensure compliance and alignment with the strategic guidance established by the Project Steering Committee. ### 50. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure  
The AE has provided a stakeholder engagement plan with the proposal, which summarizes the consultations conducted during the design and development of the project and identifies key stakeholders and their respective roles and interests in the project, including the relevant ministries, local government agencies, non-government institutions, civil society institutions, representative organizations for Indigenous Peoples, other local organizations (such as cooperatives, productive organizations) and women’s organizations.

### 4.2 Gender policy  
52. The AE has provided a gender assessment and Gender Action Plan therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  

53. The gender assessment was conducted through desk review of available data and fieldwork in which consultations were conducted with officials within project locations, while also engaging in discussion with communities with significant numbers of women and men.  

54. The gender analysis has identified key factors that are hindering women’s progress and resilience in the face of climate change. Although there is a formal institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in public institutions, the mechanisms to operationalize them are fragile. This is characterized by a high rotation of human capital and a type of management that usually lacks resources and capacities for the fulfilment of its functions. Despite these challenges, MINAM and SERNANP have shown a sustained interest in incorporating gender approaches in their actions that should be strengthened and made visible.  

Rural women are often in charge of collecting water for cooking and cleaning, using the land for livestock grazing and fetching firewood, among others. Women use and interact with natural resources and ecosystems on a daily basis and as a result are also among the first to feel the adverse effects of climate change.  

Rural women also face several barriers such as lower levels of education, inability to speak a language other than Spanish, low empowerment and leadership experience and the burden of domestic and productive work. Despite women’s important roles in small scale agriculture and water harvesting, women’s heavy domestic workload, along with social norms and resultant barriers, limit their availability, access to resources and opportunities for education, training,

72. Supply and demand risk of leveraged financing from microfinance institutions (MFIs):

Project viability and concessionality: The AE carried out the economic and financial analysis with the EUR 19.2 million of GCF grant portion to fund the establishment and operation of the Puna Facility, which has revenue generation potential. Despite those high IRR results, the AE argues that the loan option is not feasible, and grant is still required due to (1) large negative cash flow in an earlier project stage; and (2) high interest rate and margins from MFIs given the credit risk of the target beneficiaries. ### Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of  
## FP226  

### Proposal name: Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru  

#### Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ)  
#### Country: Peru  
#### Project/programme size: Medium  

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

1. This funding proposal1 for a medium-sized adaptation project in environmental and social safeguards category B is submitted by the Government of Peru through the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) as accredited entity (AE).  
2. The five executing entities (EEs) are GIZ, the Ministry of Agricultural Development and Irrigation (MIDAGRI), the National Service of Natural Areas Protected by the State (SERNANP), the Peruvian Trust Fund for National Parks and Protected Areas (Profonanpe) and Asociación Instituto Andino de Montaña (IdM). MIDAGRI and SERNANP are Government of Peru entities, Profonanpe is a private non-profit institution of public and social interest and IdM is a non-governmental organization.  
3. The total cost of the funding proposal is EUR 78.32 million, of which EUR 40.79 million is GCF finance and EUR 37.53 million is co-finance from the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development and from the Government of Peru through MIDAGRI and SERNANP.  
4. The aim of the Resilient Puna project is to increase the resilience of communities, ecosystems and livelihoods in the southern High Andes, which are negatively affected by climate change, through implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures, complemented by climate-resilient value chains, the establishment of public and private financing for investing in these measures, and support from multilevel landscape governance instruments. The project supports Quechua-speaking and other farming communities to revive ancestral knowledge on sustainable land and water management, to enhance productivity even in the face of climate change, while simultaneously maintaining crucial hydrological services for downstream water users and important stocks for global carbon regulation.  
5. The project is structured into three main components, with the outcomes as shown in the table below.  

| Component | Outcome | GCF funding |
|-----------|---------|-------------|
| 1. Resilient ecosystems and communities | Puna ecosystems are restored, conserved and better managed through the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures complemented by climate-resilient value chains | EUR 26.42 million |
| 2. Public and private investments for scaling up EbA measures | Public and private financing for EbA measures and climate-resilient livelihoods are accessible and being actively used by vulnerable communities in the Puna ecosystem | EUR 6.05 million |

1 Funding proposal package for “Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru”, received from GIZ on 15 December 2023.

## 6. The project includes the establishment and operation of the Puna Facility, implemented by Profonanpe, to deliver financing – through a call for proposals for local initiatives, led by indigenous peoples and local communities (IPLCs), that aim to increase climate resilience. The Puna Facility will fund EbA measures and climate resilient value chains, and will have three sub-windows:  

- (i) Non-Repayable Grant Sub-Window: for IPLCs with various forms of organization (local communities, producers’ associations and cooperatives in the process of formalization), applying with the support of local CSOs trusted by communities;  
- (ii) Results-based Repayable Grant Sub-Window: for micro and small enterprises, community enterprises and established cooperatives; and  
- (iii) Agroideas Matchmaking Sub-Window: for formalized producers’ organizations with commercial capacities and clear target markets, that are eligible for the MIDAGRI support programme Agroideas2.

9. Climate change mitigation through avoided emissions of greenhouse gases (GHGs) as a result of project interventions, estimated at 407,657 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO₂ eq) over 15 years, is considered as a co-benefit of the project, and is discussed under section 1.3 below.  

10. The funding proposal outlines the major climate change related hazards characterizing the southern High Andes of Peru. Increased temperatures have contributed to glacier melting, while changing rainfall patterns, including longer periods of drought, are affecting water availability in an area where 80 per cent of the population is heavily reliant on rain-fed agriculture. Ongoing climate change is combined with unsustainable productive practices that are degrading the Puna ecosystems (peatlands, wetlands or bofedales, and grasslands) and the services they provide (provision and regulation of water; provision of fodder, food and fibre; nutrient and carbon regulation).  

11. The project will work in 91 districts in the regions of Puno, Cusco, Arequipa, Apurimac and Lima, covering 5,314,607 ha. These districts comprise high-altitude Andean areas (over 3,500 m above sea-level), or are in a buffer zone above 2,800 m, where vulnerable communities practice crop and livestock farming in Puna ecosystems with high or very high risks of droughts and other climate hazards, and including degraded lands and areas that have suffered melting of glaciers. Of the 91 districts, 58 will be eligible for the Puna Facility (covering 4,116,475 ha) and capacity-building activities, while 33 districts will benefit only from capacity-building activities. Four natural protected areas managed by SERNANP fall inside eligible districts: Nor Yauyos-Cochas Landscape Reserve in the Lima region; Cotahuasi Subbasin Landscape Reserve and Salinas y Aguada Blanca National Reserve in the Arequipa region; and Ampay National Sanctuary in the Apurimac region.  

12. The project aims to directly benefit 60,715 people (of whom 30,088 will be female). This number is made up of:  
(a) Individuals who implement local initiatives financed and supported by the Puna Facility, multiplied by the average number of members of the groups/legal entities supported and the average number of members per household in the region, representing 31,750 people;  
(b) Individuals who will be supported by MIDAGRI co-finance programmes, multiplied by the average number of members per household in the region, representing 26,315 people;  
(c) Individuals who will receive technical assistance from the GCF-funded intervention (e.g. training, support to develop site-specific climate diagnostics and preparation of participatory intervention plans), representing 1,740 people; and  
(d) Public officials who will receive capacity-building for adjusting regulations and norms, representing 910 people.  

13. The project will also indirectly benefit 2,011,856 people (of whom 1,005,928 are female) who will benefit from water generated by the improvement of ecosystems that contribute to water regulation in the upper and lower parts of watersheds, including residents of major downstream cities such as Cusco, Arequipa, Abancay and Cañete.  

14. Site-level activities will engage local farming communities, including Indigenous Quechua Peoples, farm households, producer associations and agricultural cooperatives, micro, small and medium-sized enterprises, and local government. Adaptation benefits will accrue to vulnerable communities and downstream water users, through EbA and climate-resilient value chain interventions in component 1 of the project, to be sustainably financed and embedded in governance regimes through interventions in components 2 and 3 respectively. # GCF/B.38/02/Add.06  
Page 145  

## Ecosystem-based adaptation  
The funding proposal highlights seven key EbA measures and shows how each reduces vulnerability to specific climate hazards. The measures are conservation and restoration of bofedales (wetland ecosystems), improvement of family qochas (natural reservoirs), agroforestry, forest restoration with native species, restoration of andenes (agricultural terraces), integrated soil fertility management, infiltration ditches, contour farming, conservation agriculture and sustainable grassland management. Enhancing the likely impact of these measures in the landscape is the excellent resource in the feasibility study annexed to the funding proposal, outlining restoration techniques, costs and goals in great detail. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) suggests that this could usefully be made into a manual for project implementers and producers, translated into Spanish and Quechua.  

## Climate-resilient value chains  
Support to climate-resilient value chains includes a comprehensive approach to enhance the productivity and climate resilience of production systems (supported by the EbA measures), as well as technical assistance for value addition. Target value chains are alpaca and vicuña fibres, potato and Andean grains, with complementary chains including traditional Andean crops, breeding of small animals (guinea pigs), community-based tourism and traditional handicrafts. Technical assistance is provided to access and implement Puna Facility funded local initiatives (both EbA and climate-resilient value chains, business development and access to finance). The component also involves facilitating access to MIDAGRI technical assistance and the promotion of market access for new or expanded products.

### 17. The iTAP assesses the impact potential of the project as high.  
#### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential                        Scale: Medium to high  

### 18. The proposed project is designed to bring about a paradigm shift away from the current situation of continuing degradation of Puna ecosystems of the southern High Andes region, with declining agricultural productivity and downstream water provision (and increasing GHG emissions), leaving communities increasingly vulnerable to climate change. Under the project, a new approach is developed in which communities, as stewards of the Puna, are supported to manage the ecosystems sustainably, adapt to a changing climate and break the cycle of poverty and degradation. This requires a transformation of practices by farming communities, and a new approach by government in supporting them to become productive and ecologically sustainable, even in the face of a changing climate.  

### 19. Sustainability of results. Central to achieving a sustained effect, beyond the project interventions on the ground in component 1, is the establishment of mechanisms for long-term financing of EbA and climate-resilient value chains in component 2, through three approaches:  
1. Establishing the Puna Facility and working to ensure its long-term viability;  
2. Leveraging public funds from existing payments for watershed services (MERESE) schemes; and  
3. Increasing producers’ access to Agroideas, Sierra Azul and other public programmes that promote investment in nature-based solutions, business development, product transformation and value addition in target value chains.  

### 20. Upscaling and replication. The potential for replicating the project approach in other parts of the High Andes is strong, and is set up by component 3, enhancing the enabling environment for support to climate-resilient agriculture and value chains, and developing the policy and regulatory framework. The creation of an enabling environment relies on transformational planning and programming to scale up EbA, integrating community-responsive technologies, ancestral practices and landscape interventions into local, regional and national planning processes. Also important in this regard are the investments in public institutional capacity and coordination to mainstream EbA through national and regional government. Development of regulatory frameworks is achieved through mainstreaming of EbA and gender considerations within the daily operations of MIDAGRI, MINAM and SERNANP; improving norms, laws, budget and investment guidelines to enhance EbA and climate-resilient approaches in agriculture and land-use planning; and feeding into and strengthening the national monitoring and evaluation systems to allow the incorporation of EbA.  

### 21. Knowledge and learning. Knowledge has a very central role in the project strategy, which emphasizes the value of traditional practices (combined with modern scientific knowledge). A key part of the EbA approach is consciously reviving and disseminating ancient practices that are crucial for adapting to climate change, for example:  
1. Storing rainwater in micro water reservoirs (qochas in Quechua) for recharging aquifers;  
2. Constructing stonework irrigation channels to bring water from high-altitude rivers and qochas to irrigate pastures or create watering troughs; and  
3. Holding annual festivals for communal clearing of irrigation channels (as well as vicuña shearing and other communal activities).  

Although much knowledge has been lost through what the funding proposal describes as “low valuation of native cultures, cultural erosion, high rates of rural-urban migration”, knowledge is still present, especially in some Quechua communities of Cusco, Apurimac and Puno, who retain forms of organization

22. The iTAP welcomes the inclusion of targeted activities in output 1.2 to make this knowledge revival possible. Sub-activity 1.2.1.1 builds the capacity of local experts for the transfer of ancestral and innovative knowledge linked to EbA and climate-resilient value chains measures, involving yachachiqs, kamayocs, talentos rurales and rural extensionists, in a capacity-building programme and in transferring knowledge to communities and the technical teams of national and subnational institutions. This includes knowledge of ancestral and innovative techniques for small irrigation systems, installation of greenhouses, animal breeding, cultivating pastures and growing crops. Other sub-activities facilitate knowledge-sharing among beneficiary communities with a gender perspective, and disseminate information materials in accessible formats and language.  

23. Sub-activity 1.2.1.4, “Facilitating intergenerational dialogue on ancestral practices and innovation in a changing context”, led by IdM and working through local civil society organizations, is an important intervention. It is surprising, however, that no other project activities focus on youth, or target them for training on climate-resilient value chains, to counter the trends of out-migration and erosion of knowledge. A full revival of traditional practices will not be possible without more systematic engagement of the next generation, and the iTAP recommends that youth who are present in the target regions, while not necessarily being active in farming, should be included in the EbA, climate-resilient value chains, entrepreneurial and financial training under sub-activity 1.1.3.1.   

24. It is welcomed that the project will collect and analyse information on the impact of EbA measures, to improve evidence of EbA effectiveness and inform future decision-making processes, through sub-activity 1.2.2.3. Studies and research will be conducted using data generated by the community monitoring committees, for example on impact measurement of EbA (carbon sequestration, ecosystem recovery and water regulation), cost–benefit analysis, cost risk reduction in business operations, cost-effectiveness and the type of ecosystems to be investigated (e.g. peatlands, wetlands, grasslands). This is important as part of building the evidence base for the CGF global investment in EbA approaches.  

25. Overall, based on the above, the paradigm shift potential of the project is assessed as medium to high.

# GCF/B.38/02/Add.06  
## Page 148  

### 27. Environmental co-benefits  
As a climate change mitigation co-benefit, GHG emissions will be avoided through the conservation, restoration and sustainable management of Puna ecosystems across 23,914 ha. The proposed intervention measures aim to prevent emissions from existing carbon sources through inappropriate management practices in livestock and crop farming, and contribute to carbon sequestration in soil and vegetation, through grassland management, forest restoration, integrated soil fertility management, agroforestry and wetlands restoration and conservation. Anticipated GHG reductions are calculated using the methodology presented in the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry, applied through the tool EX-ACT (version 9.4) developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations. The overall expected mitigation impact is a reduction in GHG emissions of 407,657 t CO2 eq in the 15 years analyzed (5-year implementation phase and 10-year capitalization phase), measured against a scenario without the project interventions (see funding proposal, annex 22b).  

### 28. In addition to carbon sequestration, the project will enhance biodiversity and ecosystem functioning – protecting and restoring peatlands, wetlands and grasslands, which provide a habitat for a range of fauna, as well as ecosystem services – including soil formation, nutrient recycling, water regulation, water purification, local climate regulation, water retention and storage, groundwater recharge and discharge, recovery and removal of excess nutrients and pollutants, and a peat blanket protecting the underlying soils from erosion.  

### 29. Socioeconomic co-benefits  
Awareness-raising and capacity-building of local stakeholders, including women, on EbA and value chain strengthening will improve social capital and cohesion. Ancient knowledge and local expertise will be integrated through a participatory approach. Activities to enhance the productivity of farming and grazing should improve household income and nutritional status, and the iTAP welcomes the inclusion of an indicator in the logical framework to measure this co-benefit.  
New livelihoods based on climate-resilient value chains are expected to bring about local economic development, with new jobs and markets being created. Reducing the barriers to access to finance for expanding production and agri-processing through the Puna Facility will also promote rural prosperity. The extent to which grants and repayable grants (flexible, zero-interest loans) will promote financial literacy and the development of a track record that can enable future access to microfinance remains to be seen (see section 1.6 below).  

### 30. Gender co-benefits  
Engagement of women in project livelihood opportunities and decision-making will promote women’s empowerment. The mainstreaming of a gender approach within all the project activities will promote gender equality and enhance the living conditions and economic opportunities of Andean women. This is achieved through promoting local initiatives (including EbA measures and climate-resilient value chains) with a gender-sensitive approach, integrating gender issues in platforms of knowledge exchange for ancestral practices and increasing women’s participation in watershed dialogue decision-making platforms. For this purpose, the project includes a gender action plan with specific activities and an indicator for co-benefit 5 “Gender responsive approaches mainstreamed in EbA and CRVC measures”.  

### 31. The iTAP assesses the sustainable development potential of the project as high.

Scale: High  

### 32. Peru’s southern High Andes region is highly vulnerable to climate change, and is expected to experience a continuing trend of increased maximum temperatures, decreased

# GCF/B.38/02/Add.06  
## Page 149  

Climate change impacts are compounding already unsustainable farming practices by agropastoral communities, worsening vulnerability. A decline in the once-widespread traditional knowledge used to sustainably manage the natural resources that support Andean livelihoods, combined with continued lack of access to agricultural extension, finance and markets, are causing declining agricultural productivity and incomes, with young people leaving the mountains to seek work in cities. In some areas, this loss of labour has caused households to shift from crop farming to less labour-intensive cattle farming, accelerating the cycle of degradation of the native grasslands. This is compounded by higher temperatures allowing the introduction of sheep and cattle for extensive grazing in parts of the region where previously only lower-impact South American camelids were roaming.

Today, the once biodiverse and highly productive Puna ecosystems are vulnerable to overgrazing, uncontrolled expansion of the agricultural frontier, poor management of grasslands and degradation of peatlands and wetlands (also causing GHG emissions).  

The target agropastoralist communities in the southern High Andes of Peru region are highly vulnerable to climate change because of their reliance on rain-fed agriculture and extensive grazing. This is compounded by poverty and underdevelopment – 73 per cent of the southern High Andes of Peru districts have a low or very low Human Development Index. Local communities are geographically dispersed, lack basic services such as healthcare, potable water, sanitation education and financing, and rely on wood, dung and peat as energy sources. Poverty is compounded by limited access to existing government agricultural support programmes, and access to finance often only at exorbitant costs via informal lenders known as “drop by drop” or “express loans”, with low penetration by rural microfinance institutions.  

Downstream communities that rely on the mountain catchments’ water regulatory services to maintain flow for hydropower, domestic and industrial use are also vulnerable to climate change worsened by poor catchment management. Increased temperatures have already contributed to the melting of 51 per cent of the region’s glaciers, while changing rainfall patterns and longer drought periods have affected water availability, with decreases in overall rainfall projected for southern Peru. In addition, huge stocks of carbon stored in the Puna ecosystems could be released into the atmosphere if they continue degrading, with global consequences.  

Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are rated as high.  
### 1.5 Country ownership  
Scale: High  

The Resilient Puna project has been prioritized by the Ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF), as Peru’s nationally designated authority, for presentation to GCF. It has the full support of the Peruvian Government through MEF, MIDAGRI, as the ministry leading the project, and MINAM, which leads climate change action in Peru.

### 38. Policy and strategy alignment:  
The project is closely aligned with the targets set out in Peru’s nationally determined contribution,15 supporting the fulfilment of four adaptation measures related to agriculture and water:  
(i) AGRI7, “Natural grassland management to secure feed for livestock and reduce vulnerability to climate change”;  
(ii) AGRI11, “Management of wild South American camelids (vicuñas) considering effects of climate change”;  
(iii) AGRI16, “Implementation of business strategies addressing risks and opportunities in the face of climate change”; and  
(iv) AGU2, “Interventions on planting and water harvesting for agricultural water security in watersheds vulnerable to climate change”.  

The iTAP welcomes the attempt by the AE to quantify the project’s potential contribution to these four targets (see table 21 in annex 2a to the funding proposal), and hopes the actual results will be tracked and fed into the national monitoring and evaluation system for reporting to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change secretariat on adaptation.  

### 39. The project will support the implementation of Peru’s national adaptation plan through focusing on ecosystems, hydrographic watersheds, restoration and conservation of ecosystems, recovering traditional knowledge and territorial planning, as well as microfinancing for the promotion of EbA practices. The project is also aligned with the National Agrarian Policy 2021–2030 and the Plan for Risk Management and Adaptation to Climate Change in the Agricultural Sector for 2012–2021.  

The establishment of the Puna Facility and the support to climate-resilient value chains are well aligned with the Road Map for Green Financing, the National Strategy for Green Growth and the objectives of the National Competitiveness and Productivity Plan 2019–2030.  

### 40. Roles of key entities:  
The roles of the key entities in overseeing or implementing the project, and their relevant experience, capacity or mandate to play these roles, are set out below. It is noted that two separate entities of GIZ are involved in the project, playing distinct roles, which will not overlap.  

#### (a) GIZ:  
GIZ is a German-based private, limited liability, non-profit company owned by the Federal Republic of Germany; It is serving as AE, providing oversight of the project. GIZ, as an AE to GCF since 2016, has already developed and submitted several funding proposals to GCF, with six approved proposals under implementation (FP117, FP132, FP103, FP059, FP198 and FP200) and a seventh starting soon (FP214). GIZ is one of the largest international providers of capacity development and technical assistance on climate change worldwide.  

#### (b) GIZ Peru:  
With its management structure based in Peru, GIZ Peru is the main EE, and is responsible, either alone or with other EEs, for the execution of all the project activities except for activity 1.1.2. MIDAGRI, SERNANP, Profonanpe and IdM will also serve as EEs.  

#### (c) MIDAGRI:  
MIDAGRI has extensive experience of channelling loan and grant resources for national and subnational rural development projects from international financial institutions. It is currently implementing several projects from the International Fund for Agricultural Development, the World Bank and the Inter-American Development Bank. The project will enable target communities to access a number of existing MIDAGRI programmes, which form co-finance to the project (see section 1.6 below).  

#### (d) Profonanpe:  
A private non-profit organization, Profonanpe is the EE responsible for the implementation of activities 1.1.2 (with MIDAGRI), 1.1.3 (with GIZ Peru) and 2.2.1 (with GIZ Peru). Profonanpe’s capacity is indicated by its accreditation as a national direct access entity by GCF since 2015, with two projects under implementation as AE and EE (FP001 and FP193). Profonanpe’s experience in designing and managing several

# 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

## Scale: Medium

45. Of the project’s total proposed budget of EUR 78.32 million, 52 per cent (EUR 40.79 million) is proposed to be a GCF grant, and 48 per cent (EUR 37.53 million) is made up of co-financing to the project, closely woven into the project logic, and broken down as follows:

(a) MIDAGRI will contribute EUR 28.67 million through grants (EUR 2.41 million), in-kind contributions (EUR 16.88 million) and loans (EUR 9.39 million). These will cover (i) public investments projects (implemented by the Agrarian Productive Development Program, Sierra Azul, the Subsectoral Irrigation Program, the National Institute for Agricultural Innovation and the National Forestry and Wildlife Service; (ii) operational activities (implemented by programmes such as Agroideas, providing matching funding for agribusiness); and (iii) individual credit lines to producer organizations. Supporting target communities to meet the established technical, financial or institutional requirements for these MIDAGRI programmes and funds18 is central to the project’s exit strategy;

(b) SERNANP will contribute in-kind contributions worth EUR 0.95 million to the implementation of the project in the Arequipa, Apurimac and Lima regions, including staff time contributions in order to coordinate project interventions in NPAs;

(c) Co-financing from the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development for the project will be fully integrated into the project activities, with a total grant amount of EUR 7.91 million. EUR 6.26 million will be implemented through GIZ as an EE for eight activities; EUR 1.5 million will be forwarded to Profonanpe for the Puna Facility and EUR 150,000 to IdM.

46. In addition to the co-finance included in the funding proposal, the project aims to mobilize additional resources from international donors and the private sector to support Profonanpe in ensuring the sustainability of the Puna Facility (through sub-activity 2.1.1.2). The Government of Canada has already expressed interest in contributing approximately EUR 6 million, particularly for financing gender and monitoring related activities, and replenishing the Puna Facility to continue financing local initiatives, although this is still to be confirmed.

47. Level of concessionality. For the elements of the project unrelated to the Puna Facility, that is, investment in public goods for EbA and strengthening of the enabling environment, the 100 per cent grant from GCF seems justified. The GCF investment in implementing, financing and scaling up EbA represents a cost-effective and efficient integrated landscape approach to obtaining adaptation benefits and mitigation co-benefits. There is a growing body of evidence, including from High Andes interventions, on the cost-effectiveness of EbA approaches to addressing climate change, to which the proposed project will contribute.19 Other parts of the project involve private goods, for example Puna Facility investments in family crop farming or agribusiness micro, small and medium-sized enterprises, where profits will be made, although probably with relatively small margins (see discussion below on financial rate of return).

48. Economic rate of return. The economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of the project as a whole is 8.2 per cent, which is more than two percentage points above the 6 per cent Peru

## Page 153  

### 49. Financial rate of return and choice of financial instruments  
The financial rates of return on climate-resilient production of the key products of native potatoes and alpacas are estimated by the AE as shown in the table below.  

| Local initiative type | Financial internal rate of return without grant | Financial internal rate of return with grant |
|-----------------------|--------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|
| Non-repayable – Native potatoes | 12.6% | 121.8% |
| Non-repayable – Alpacas | 8.4% | 63.7% |
| Repayable – Native potatoes | 25.8% | 84.3% |
| Repayable – Alpacas | 17.1% | 48.1% |
| Agroideas – Alpacas | 19.5% | 56.8% |

### 50. These potential rates of return, calculated for the financial analysis, appear to the iTAP to have been overestimated, creating an apparent mismatch between the potential profitability of these key agricultural activities and the possibility of funding them through pure grants from the Puna Facility.  

The iTAP acknowledges that the principle underlying the three sub-windows is related to the segmentation of the potential beneficiaries by organizational and commercial capacities and the level of barriers they face. However, it seems short-sighted not to also take into account the potential profitability of activities to be invested in. Pure grants may indeed make sense for collective EbA investments in the landscape. But offering a pure grant to a producers’ association that is expecting a high financial return on its activity represents a missed opportunity – both to maximize the development of the association’s financial track record and to generate a return for replenishing the facility.  

### 51. The non-repayable grant window is currently proposed to form 52 per cent of the total funds in the Puna Facility. The iTAP believes that the total volume of pure (non-repayable) grants should be as small as possible, with such grants reserved for local initiatives that are unlikely to generate significant profits.  

The iTAP notes that GIZ is not accredited with GCF for on-lending, but believes that the design of the facility to cover only pure grants and repayable grants (that are effectively zero-interest loans, with highly flexible repayment terms) means that the effect on promoting recipients’ financial literacy and financial inclusion will be limited. Ideally, it would have been preferable for the facility to have included regular low-interest, favourable-term (long tenor, long grace period) loans, in order to maximize eligible participants’ capacity to understand the principles of risk and repayment, develop their financial literacy, enhance their prospects of accessing future commercial microfinance, and help to break the cycle of poverty.  

### 52. The iTAP recommends that the AE and Profonanpe should think strategically about the balance between pure grants and repayable grants offered through the Puna Facility. Ideally, the project should apply the principle of minimum concessionality, and avoid giving pure grants to

# GCF/B.38/02/Add.06  
## Page 154  

53. It is noted that the project explicitly addresses barriers to beneficiaries’ future access to commercial finance though a sub-activity under output 2.1 on “Support to financial institutions in greening microcredits” – working with microfinance institutions with a presence in the project area and with interest on expanding their credit portfolio to small-scale farmers for adaptation measures (e.g. Caja Rural los Andes, Caja Cusco, and Financiera Confianza – an entity of BBVA Microfinance Foundation) and providing technical assistance to adjust existing products (including for women) or develop new ones, incorporating the EbA approach in the financial risk analysis for prioritized value chains. Another sub-activity aims to “Raise awareness of greener microcredits to promote EbA and Climate Resilient Value Chains” through an awareness campaign for potential clients, training for field personnel of financial institutions, and cooperation in strengthening risk management approaches. This needs to be complemented by in-depth entrepreneurial and financial training in sub-activity 1.1.3.1 – currently described only in broad terms.  

54. Replenishing the facility. Although the project proponents anticipate some return on the repayable grants, this amount is expected to be only 50 per cent of the total disbursed, and applies to only 48 per cent (windows 2 and 3) of the portfolio as currently structured. In effect, much of the Puna Facility operates like a small grants facility, and seems unlikely to attract private sector investors who would expect a conventional return. The AE has acknowledged that a special strategy is required for replenishment, and has designed sub-activity 2.1.1.2 to address this: “Development and implementation of an innovative strategy to mobilize resources from the private sector and different donors”. This activity focuses on accessing funding from the private sector and other donors, and includes mapping and engagement of potential private sector actors, both those interested in corporate social responsibility investment in the Andes and those whose businesses depend on the ecosystem services provided by the Puna ecosystems, such as hydroelectric, cement, mining, beverages and camelid fibre products, who might wish to invest in the facility.  

55. One-off contributions from donors or companies, however, would not constitute a long-term replenishment strategy for the Puna Facility. Two more sustainable options exist and could be explicitly addressed in the strategy to be developed for the facility. One option, which may be implied in the funding proposal, is for Profonanpe to reach long-term agreements with companies dependent on upper catchment management for water provision such as hydropower or beverage industries – in effect a type of payment for watershed services scheme. A second option could be a long-term source of State revenue channelled into the facility by law – such as a portion of the national Tax on Consumption of Plastic Bags or the Aporte por Regulación contribution by mining, hydrocarbon, electricity and other companies to the Environmental Assessment and Control Agency. The iTAP recommends that a full business plan should be developed in sub-activity 2.1.1.2, explicitly targeting long-term agreements and State revenue sources.  

56. Payment for Watershed Services. The efficiency and effectiveness of the project interventions is further enhanced by sub-activity 2.1.2.1, which will help to activate Peru’s existing payment for watershed services scheme, MERESE, bringing additional resources into the target regions.23 The project will provide technical assistance to water utilities for five existing MERESE schemes in Arequipa, Lima, Cusco and Apurimac (details can be found in annex 23 to the funding proposal). Technical assistance packages may include support in planning, evaluation and selection of EbA public investment projects; strengthening governance platforms; contributing to the design of the monitoring, gender and capacity development  

23 Not directly linked to the Puna Facility, given that MERESE schemes are clearly circumscribed in how they operate.

# GCF/B.38/02/Add.06  
## Page 155  

The project’s overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated as medium.  

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

The iTAP believes that the project “Resilient Puna: Ecosystem based Adaptation for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru” has been well planned, consulted and designed by GIZ and the Government of Peru. The project is an important one for reducing the vulnerability of High Andean agropastoral communities and downstream water users, and contributing to arresting an ongoing cycle of poverty and degradation that would otherwise lead to ecosystem collapse and significant GHG emissions.  

The iTAP recommends that the AE undertake the following actions to strengthen the sustainability of project results:  

1. Include youth who are present in the target regions, while not necessarily being active in farming, in the entrepreneurial training (including financial literacy) through the technical assistance facility for the Puna Facility (sub-activity 1.1.3.1);  
2. Consider carefully the balance between pure grants and repayable grants offered through the Puna Facility, and apply the principle of minimum concessionality when supporting profit-making activities (sub-activity 2.1.1.1); and  
3. Develop a full business plan for the Puna Facility, explicitly targeting long-term agreements with private sector partners, and long-term sources of revenue, including from national environmental taxes and payments (sub-activity 2.1.1.2).

# Chapter 1: Introduction

The “Resilient Puna: Ecosystem-based approaches for sustainable High Andean communities and ecosystems in Peru” Project (hereafter “Resilient Puna”) aims to increase the resilience of Andean communities in the departments of Arequipa, Cusco, Puno and Apurimac, through the management, conservation and restoration of high Andean ecosystems; an increased access to public and private financing; and a stronger territorial planning towards Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA). It is organised into three components: (i) Puna ecosystems are restored, conserved and better managed to support climate resilient livelihoods, through the implementation of EbAs measures ; (ii) Public and private financing for EbA measures and climate resilient livelihoods are in place and accessible for vulnerable communities in the Puna Ecosystem; and, (iii) EbA and climate resilience are mainstreamed into multilevel landscape governance instruments. The project will invest in EbA and climate resilient value chains, providing technical support to communities and producer associations to achieve sustainable business plans; directly benefiting the conservation and restoration of 23,914 hectares of high Andean ecosystems.

Various international and national instruments have defined guidelines for gender equality in key climate change mitigation and adaptation actions. Not only from a social, but also from an economic and climate perspective. The link between gender, environment and climate encompasses a number of issues based on the socially constructed relationships between men and women, which vary across cultures, geographies and time. Gender gaps are still wide and trigger inequalities in access to their basic rights, opportunities and benefits throughout their life cycle, which are reflected in their health, education, employment, participation, decision-making power, access to and control of natural resources, capabilities, living a life free of violence, among others. Climate change represents a major driver of biodiversity loss, while ecosystem degradation undermines the resilience of people and nature to climate-related shocks and stressors. Persistent gender inequalities underpin both of these crises, which increase vulnerability to risks and reduce the options available to people, especially women, in maintaining their livelihoods and good living, and their access to rights, goods and services.

The gender analysis of the Resilient Puna (RP) Project is in harmony with these international principles and commitments (CEDAW, Beijing, Belém do Pará, SDGs), as well as with national ones (Gender and Climate Change Action Plan -PACC, National Policy on Gender Equality, National Strategic Development Plan to 2050, National Plan against Gender Violence, among other policies) that support work in favour of gender equality and non-discrimination of women in the access to their social, political, cultural, climatic and economic rights and resources.

According to the United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) (2017), the impacts of global warming that affect men and women differently, require adaptation and mitigation measures that recognise their different needs. It also implies promoting the mainstreaming of a gender perspective in public policies to address climate change, with the aim of observing, analysing and transforming inequalities and inequities in the condition, construction of roles and position of men and women in society (UN-Women, 2017).

The project has adopted as its main approach "Ecosystem-based Adaptation" (EbA), an approach that promotes working with nature to cope with gradual and extreme climate impacts, which affect people, their livelihoods and their economy. In this approach, two central characteristics are identified in order to better understand the gender approach:

1. It is a people-based approach, as it seeks to reduce their vulnerability while at the same time generating social and economic benefits; and,

3 "The public need for a National Gender Equality Policy aimed at correcting the problem of structural discrimination against women stems both from constitutional norms and the Peruvian State's commitment to human rights treaties...". PNIG, 2019.

# 48

## ii) it is a collaborative process in which communities, public and private organisations, work together to have an impact at the landscape level and generate sustainable opportunities over time for all parties.

Linking the gender EbA approaches is both challenging and enriching. It allows to move from analysing the resources, decisions, behaviours, roles and interrelationships of men and women with their environment, towards integrating ways and mechanisms to break down the barriers that have been hindering women's progress in the framework of their adaptation and resilience to climate change.

Specifically, the gender analysis has identified the main factors that are hindering women's progress and resilience in the face of climate change. Overburdened care and unpaid domestic work; low participation in decision-making; lack of income; vulnerability of their housing and household environment (e.g., health, food, gender-based violence, poor housing) are the main gendered barriers to more equitable climate adaptation. Although the response to this situation requires the involvement of both women and men, it is important to mention that the gender dimension addressed in the project defines actions that mainly strengthen the role of women, seeking to "levelling the playing field" for their real and effective involvement in the implementation of the project, as well as a better appropriation of the EbA issue and taking advantage of the opportunities and benefits provided by the project.

The project recognises that an adequate vision and inclusion of gender aspects in its different stages is one of the main pillars for the effectiveness and sustainability of the intervention. For this reason, both the Gender Analysis (GA) and the Gender Action Plan (GAP) of the Resilient Puna project focus on the mainstreaming of the gender approach from its formulation to its implementation, monitoring and evaluation. Therefore, the RP project will also conduct a monitoring strategy to evaluate and verify that no negative results are produced (under the principle of do no harm). The project will encourage women's participation and leadership to be promoted and recognised in the environments in which the project will be developed, in many of which they have been traditionally excluded. The central objective of the analysis and integration of the gender approach in the project is visibilise women's barriers to contribute reducing asymmetric situations in the care, exploitation and use of natural resources, as well as the rights of people, especially women, within a natural ecosystem. In this sense, having a reading of the gender approach in this area leads us to build egalitarian processes that allow people to build climate resilience and exercise their rights, while protecting the ecosystems on which they depend.

## Finally, the methodology to collect information to prepare this gender action plan involved a desk review of available data and fieldwork data collection techniques. Regarding desk review, it included analysis of available secondary data sources such as national policies and work of national mechanisms, as well as a comprehensive review of secondary literature that includes various research reports, articles and papers on the subject from both national and international organisations. Regarding fieldwork data collection, between August and November 2022, two fieldtrips were organised to potential districts of intervention in the departments of Arequipa, Cusco and Puno. During these fieldtrips, personnel from GIZ, MIDAGRI and FIAP conducted focus groups, in-depth interviews and surveys to analyse general perceptions about the Resilient Puna project, governance structures regarding management of natural resources, local livelihoods and gender gaps in rural communities.

To address gender topics, 89 in-depth interviews were conducted, of which 40 were with women and 49 with men. The greater number of interviews with men was due to their greater willingness to be interviewed. Likewise, surveys were applied to build a baseline that gathers evidence and statistical data, for the definition of indicators of the Gender Action Plan of the project. Likewise, the baseline reflects as close reality of the situation of women and men at the territorial level as well as the actors involved in the context in which they develop.

# Chapter 2: Information on gender dimensions in the country or the region  
## 2.1 Meta level (norms) 

Peru is the fourth country with the largest population in South America. In 2020, the population was 32.6 million inhabitants: 50.4% women and 49.6% men. This population lives mostly in urban centres (79%) and 21% in rural areas (INEI, 2020b). Peru also hosts great cultural diversity with 55 indigenous peoples, 51 from the Amazon and 4 from the Andes (Ministerio de Cultura, 2022). According to the last National Population Census (2017), 26% of the population identifies as part of an indigenous group, which makes a round 6 million people(INEI, 2017).

Moreover, Peru is a country with high levels of inequality, in terms of income, property, access to basic social services, infrastructure and gender. In 2020, monetary poverty increased by 9.9 percentage points and reached 30% of the Peruvian population. This affected 46% of people living in rural areas (3.1 million people) and 26% of people living in urban areas (6.8 million people) (INEI, 2021b).

In terms of gender inequity, Peru has progressed launching different norms for the mainstreaming of a gender approach in public policy, but it is still insufficient. The most recent 2020-21 World Economic Forum (WEF) Global Gender Gap Index placed Peru 37th out of 156 countries in terms of gender gaps pertaining to economic participation and opportunity, education, health and survival, and political empowerment dimensions (WEF, 2020 -2021) and 7th place in Latin America. Similarly, the Gender Inequality Index in 2019 placed Peru with 0.395 points, a position below the average of countries with high human development (0.340) (UNDP, 2022).

Peru is a state that prohibits discrimination and recognises equality as a fundamental human right (Political Constitution of 1993, Art. 2 numeral 2). Article 191 of the Constitution establishes the responsibility of the Peruvian State to remove obstacles that limit the right to equality or that constitute discriminatory acts. However, there is structural discrimination against women in Peru, which is systematic, generalised, and massive. This discrimination generates multiple oppressions for women, limiting their opportunities and systematically violating their rights. Women are affected by multiple discriminations that are evident in the persistence of limitations to their autonomy (physical, economic and decision-making), as well as the existence of gaps in different dimensions (reproductive health, empowerment and labour market) and areas (health, education, employment, participation, use of time, among others), with the situation worsening in the rural settings. Structural discrimination based on gender does not affect all women equally, who do not constitute a homogeneous group, since there are indigenous, Afro-Peruvian, disable, displaced, poor and non-poor women.

The supremacy of the masculine over the feminine is institutionalised in social relations, in public and private institutions and in politics, through discriminatory sociocultural patterns (beliefs, prejudices, dogmas, and customs), which are reproduced, transmitted intergenerationally and, therefore, perpetuated. The origin of these sociocultural norms lies in the differences in power between women and men and male privilege. Discrimination against women is rooted in norms and an institutional culture that reinforce women's subordination. Violence against women is one of the main instruments used to maintain women in a situation of subordination and as a means of disciplining them to prevent their access to rights. This not only prevents their participation in social, political, economic, and cultural decision-making processes, but also makes it impossible for them to transform these spaces that reproduce patriarchal structures of domination.

According to the National Social Relations Survey in 2019 (ENARES, for its acronym in Spanish), the index of social tolerance towards violence against women is 59% of the surveyed. When referring to attitudes that support such violence, 53% of male and female respondents

Gender inequality has an impact on development and the economy, but above all on families’  
wellbeing, where unpaid labour and care roles are still locked by stereotypes, and stigmas, in  
which women tend to have a heavier burden than men and fewer opportunities to achieve their  
wellbeing. In the context of confinement due to the Covid 19 pandemic, there was a difference  
of 19 percentage points between men and women, regarding the time spen t on household

market under similar conditions as their male counterparts. In 2022, 82% of women perform

27 hours more on unpaid domestic work per week (Flora Tristan & IEP, 2021).  

By positioning the gender approach with key partners throughout the project implementation
